The **softmax** turns a score for each class into probabilities that add to one: exponentiate each score, divide by the total. Minimising log loss is maximum likelihood, exactly as for a logit; a network classifier is a multinomial logit whose inputs are features the network built itself.

### Worked example — what a confident mistake costs

Log loss charges $-\log(\hat p)$, where $\hat p$ is the probability the model gave to what actually happened. Compare the cost of saying 0.6 and 0.01 for an event that then happens.

### Solution

$-\log(0.6)$ = 0.51 and $-\log(0.01)$ = 4.61: the confident mistake costs 9 times as much. A network trained on log loss therefore learns to hedge when it is unsure, which is why its probabilities can be used for decisions (Chapter 12). Mean squared error on a probability would charge only $(1 - 0.01)^2 \approx 0.98$ against $(1 - 0.6)^2 = 0.16$, about six times as much, and is much more forgiving of overconfidence.

## Gradient descent and backpropagation

There is no formula for the best weights of a network, as there is for OLS, so we search. **Gradient descent** (Chapter 10) does it by walking downhill: compute the slope of the loss with respect to every weight (the **gradient**), step each weight a little in the downhill direction, repeat. The step size is the **learning rate**, the most important setting in neural-network training: too small and training crawls, too large and it overshoots and diverges.

Three refinements make this work at scale. **Mini-batches**: instead of computing the gradient on all the data at each step, estimate it from a random batch of, say, 64 rows; the estimate is noisy but cheap, and many cheap steps beat a few expensive ones. One pass through the training data is an **epoch**. **Adam**, the default optimiser almost everywhere, adapts the step size separately for each weight. And unlike OLS, the loss has many valleys, so different random starting weights give different networks. Fix the seed, and never interpret an individual weight.

How is the gradient computed for thousands of weights at once? By **backpropagation**, which is the chain rule from calculus organised efficiently. Run the network forward and keep every intermediate value. At the output, the error is just prediction minus truth. Each output weight’s slope is that error times what fed into the weight. Then pass the error back through the output weights to the hidden units, through each unit’s activation (a ReLU passes it if the unit was on and blocks it if the unit was off), and repeat: each hidden weight’s slope is the error that reached its unit times its input. One backward pass gives every slope.

Here is a whole one-hidden-layer network in numpy, with the backward pass written out. It is the network from the course slides, and it fits log pay to standardized professional experience for a random 1,000 developers:

``` python
def relu(z): return np.maximum(0, z)

def init(p, H, rng):                                       # random starting weights
    return {"W": rng.normal(0, 1, (p, H)), "b": rng.normal(0, 1, H),
            "v": rng.normal(0, 1 / np.sqrt(H), H), "c": 0.0}

def forward_backward(prm, X, y):
    Zh = X @ prm["W"] + prm["b"]                           # weighted sums, one column per hidden unit
    Hh = relu(Zh)                                          # hidden-unit outputs
    yhat = Hh @ prm["v"] + prm["c"]                        # prediction
    d_out = (yhat - y) / len(y)                            # error at the output
    d_hid = np.outer(d_out, prm["v"]) * (Zh > 0)           # error pushed back through each ReLU
    grads = {"v": Hh.T @ d_out, "c": d_out.sum(), "W": X.T @ d_hid, "b": d_hid.sum(0)}
    return 0.5 * np.mean((yhat - y) ** 2), grads
```

``` python
sub = df.sample(1000, random_state=368)
xs = ((sub["years_code_pro"] - sub["years_code_pro"].mean()) / sub["years_code_pro"].std()).to_numpy()[:, None]
ys = np.log(sub["comp_total"]).to_numpy()
ys = ys - ys.mean()                                        # centre the outcome
nets = {}
for H in [2, 8, 32]:
    prm = init(1, H, np.random.default_rng(368))
    for step in range(3000):
        loss, g = forward_backward(prm, xs, ys)
        prm = {k: prm[k] - 0.05 * g[k] for k in prm}       # one gradient-descent step
    nets[H] = (prm, loss)
```

![One-hidden-layer networks fitted by gradient descent, against a quadratic in experience.](18_neural_networks_files/figure-commonmark/cell-10-output-1.png)

Every network finds the same shape: pay rises steeply over roughly the first decade of a career and then goes flat. Labour economists have described the concave experience profile with a quadratic since Mincer, and the dashed line shows its limits here: a parabola has to rise too slowly at first and then turn down at the end, because that is the only shape it has. The networks put a kink where the data have one. Their final losses (half the mean squared error) are 0.1043, 0.1043 and 0.1044 for 2, 8 and 32 hidden units, against 0.1094 for the quadratic. Notice that two hinges do as well as thirty-two: with one input and a simple shape, extra capacity buys nothing, a preview of the rest of the chapter.

**Going further.** For this network, with hidden sums $z = Wx + b$, hidden outputs $h = \text{ReLU}(z)$, prediction $\hat y = v^\top h + c$ and loss $\tfrac12(\hat y - y)^2$, the chain rule gives $\partial \ell / \partial v = (\hat y - y)\,h$ and $\partial \ell / \partial W = \big[(\hat y - y)\, v \odot \mathbb 1(z > 0)\big]\, x^\top$, where $\odot$ multiplies element by element. Those are the lines `d_out` and `d_hid` in the code. Libraries such as PyTorch compute these derivatives automatically for any network you can write down (“automatic differentiation”), which is why nobody writes backward passes by hand any more.

### Worked example — checking a gradient

How would you know the backward pass is right? Nudge one weight up and down by a tiny amount $\epsilon$ and see how the loss changes: the slope should be approximately $[L(w + \epsilon) - L(w - \epsilon)] / 2\epsilon$. For the first weight of an 8-unit network at its random starting values, backpropagation says 0.152767. What does the nudge say?

### Solution

With $\epsilon = 10^{-5}$ the numerical slope is 0.152767, matching backpropagation to 12 decimal places. This **gradient check** is how people debug hand-written networks. Why not always compute gradients this way? Because it takes two passes through the data *per weight*; for the 6,337-parameter pay network that is thousands of passes per step, where backpropagation needs one.

## Neural nets in scikit-learn

scikit-learn’s `MLPRegressor` and `MLPClassifier` fit multilayer perceptrons with the same `fit` and `predict` as everything else. A network needs its inputs prepared: categories one-hot encoded (a job-type code of 16 is not “bigger” than 3) and numbers standardized. Both go in a pipeline, so that cross-validation never sees test data (Chapter 9):

``` python
pre = make_column_transformer((StandardScaler(), num), (OneHotEncoder(handle_unknown="ignore"), cat))
mlp = make_pipeline(pre, MLPRegressor(hidden_layer_sizes=(64, 32), early_stopping=True,
                                      alpha=1e-2, max_iter=300, random_state=368))
mlp.fit(X_tr, y_tr)
```

Why standardize? Gradient descent uses one learning rate for every weight, and the starting weights are random numbers that assume inputs of roughly unit size. If one input runs to 50 and another to 1, no single step size suits both. Feed the network the raw columns, integer job codes and unscaled years included, and its test RMSE is 0.478 log points, against 0.434 with the pipeline. Here is the full comparison on the held-out quarter of developers:

    mean only            0.518
    ridge (one-hot)      0.430
    MLP, raw codes       0.478
    MLP (64, 32)         0.434
    gradient boosting    0.429

The network is clearly better than predicting the mean, and about as good as ridge regression on the same one-hot inputs. Gradient boosting (Chapter 15), with no scaling, no one-hot encoding and default settings, is the best of the lot, by a small margin. Hold that thought until the section on when nets win.

### Worked example — what an RMSE of 0.43 means

The best models have a test RMSE of about 0.43 log points. In dollars, what does that mean for a developer predicted to earn \$150,000?

### Solution

An error of $e$ log points multiplies or divides pay by $e^{e}$. With $e$ = 0.429, a typical miss takes \$150,000 to between about \$97,701 and \$230,295, a third below to a half above. That is a large error, and no model here does much better, because most of what determines one developer’s pay (the employer, the city, the negotiation, skill that a survey does not measure) is not in the data. A more flexible model cannot predict from information it does not have.

## Regularization: weight decay, dropout, early stopping

A network with thousands of weights and a few thousand rows will memorise the training data if you let it. To see it happen, train a wide network, with hidden layers of 256 and 128 units, on just 1,000 developers, and measure $R^2$ by four-fold cross-validation:

![Weight decay closes the gap between training and validation fit.](18_neural_networks_files/figure-commonmark/cell-16-output-1.png)

With almost no penalty, the network explains 80% of the variance in log pay on the data it trained on and -0.08 on data it did not see: a negative $R^2$, worse than predicting the average for everyone. That is memorisation. Three defences, usually used together:

- **Weight decay** adds a penalty on the sum of squared weights to the loss, exactly the ridge penalty of Chapter 11; scikit-learn calls its strength `alpha`. Raising it shrinks the training $R^2$ and lifts the validation $R^2$, which peaks at 0.26 with alpha = 30.
- **Early stopping** holds out part of the training data, watches the loss on it after each epoch, and stops when it has not improved for a while, keeping the best weights. Chapter 11 showed that stopping gradient descent early acts like a penalty; for networks it is the cheapest regularizer there is.
- **Dropout** randomly switches off each hidden unit with some probability, say 20%, at every training step, so no unit can rely on any particular other one being there. The network becomes an average over many thinned networks, a little like a random forest. It is switched off when predicting. scikit-learn does not offer it; PyTorch does, below.

Early stopping does a lot on its own. The same wide network trained on all 4,733 training developers for 200 epochs, with no penalty and no stopping, has a training RMSE of 0.288 and a test RMSE of 0.517, barely better than predicting the mean (0.518). With early stopping, it halts after 66 epochs (the best validation score came at epoch 45), and its test RMSE is 0.444.

### Worked example — reading the gap

In the validation curve, what does the distance between the two lines tell you at small alpha, and why does the training $R^2$ *fall* as alpha rises?

### Solution

The gap is the variance part of the bias–variance trade-off (Chapter 10). A large gap means the model fits noise that does not recur in new data; with a training $R^2$ of 0.80 and a validation $R^2$ below zero, the network has learned individual developers, not patterns. The penalty makes large weights expensive, so the network can no longer bend itself around each point: its training fit falls (more bias) and its validation fit rises (less variance). Past the best alpha, the penalty starts to flatten real patterns too, and both lines fall together. It is the same picture as ridge regression, because it is the same penalty.

## A first PyTorch training loop

scikit-learn’s networks are fine for small tabular problems, but you cannot change their architecture, add dropout, or use a GPU. **PyTorch** is the library research and industry use, including for every large language model in Part IV. Its four ideas are **tensors** (arrays that remember how they were computed), **autograd** (`loss.backward()` runs backpropagation for you), **modules** (layers you stack), and **optimisers** (which take the downhill steps).

The natural first example is images. scikit-learn’s `load_digits` has 1,797 handwritten digits, each an 8 × 8 grid of grey levels, so 64 numbers per image and ten classes. It is a miniature of the problem of reading handwritten figures off old census forms, firm ledgers or tax records, which is how economic historians now build datasets.

![Ten of the 1,797 handwritten digits, 8 × 8 pixels each.](18_neural_networks_files/figure-commonmark/cell-19-output-1.png)

The model: 64 inputs, one hidden layer of 128 ReLU units with 20% dropout, and 10 outputs, one score per digit (the softmax is folded into the loss).

``` python
torch.manual_seed(368)
Xt_tr, Xt_te = torch.tensor(Xd_tr, dtype=torch.float32), torch.tensor(Xd_te, dtype=torch.float32)
yt_tr, yt_te = torch.tensor(yd_tr), torch.tensor(yd_te)
model = nn.Sequential(nn.Linear(64, 128), nn.ReLU(), nn.Dropout(p=0.2), nn.Linear(128, 10))
loss_fn = nn.CrossEntropyLoss()                            # softmax + log loss
opt = torch.optim.Adam(model.parameters(), lr=1e-3, weight_decay=1e-4)
```

And the loop that trains it, which is the same loop, give or take, that trains every deep-learning model in existence:

``` python
history = []
for epoch in range(30):
    model.train()                                          # dropout on
    perm = torch.randperm(len(Xt_tr))
    for i in range(0, len(perm), 64):                      # mini-batches of 64
        idx = perm[i:i + 64]
        loss = loss_fn(model(Xt_tr[idx]), yt_tr[idx])
        opt.zero_grad(); loss.backward(); opt.step()       # clear, backpropagate, step
    model.eval()                                           # dropout off
    with torch.no_grad():
        history.append((model(Xt_te).argmax(1) == yt_te).float().mean().item())
```

![Test accuracy of the PyTorch network after each epoch.](18_neural_networks_files/figure-commonmark/cell-22-output-1.png)

The network has 9,610 weights and biases. After one epoch it reads 68% of the test digits correctly, and after thirty, 96.7%. The whole thing takes a couple of seconds on a laptop CPU. (In a real project the accuracy per epoch would be tracked on a validation set, not the test set; I show the test set here only to keep the example short.)

### Worked example — train and eval

Why does the loop call `model.train()` before each epoch and `model.eval()` before measuring accuracy? What would go wrong without the second?

### Solution

They switch dropout on and off. In training mode, each hidden unit is zeroed with probability 0.2 at every step (and the survivors scaled up to compensate), which is the regularization. In evaluation mode, every unit is used, so predictions are deterministic and use the whole network. Forget `model.eval()` and each prediction would be made by a randomly thinned network: accuracy would be lower and would change every time you asked. The same switch matters for other layers that behave differently in training, which is why it is a habit worth having from the start.

## When nets beat trees, and when they don’t

On developer pay, a neural network did no better than ridge regression and a little worse than boosted trees, after more preparation and more tuning. That is not bad luck. Grinsztajn, Oyallon and Varoquaux (2022) compared tuned networks with tuned tree ensembles on 45 medium-sized tabular datasets and found the trees ahead on most of them, with far less tuning effort. Their explanations, in plain words:

1.  **Real targets are often step-like.** Pay jumps at job titles and firm-size thresholds; trees split at thresholds naturally, while networks prefer smooth functions.
2.  **Useless columns hurt networks more.** A tree ignores a column it never splits on; a network gives every input a weight and has to learn to set the useless ones to zero.
3.  **Spreadsheet columns mean something on their own.** Income, age and firm size are meaningful individually; a dense layer mixes every column with every other from the first step, while a tree uses one meaningful column at a time.

The second is easy to test. Add 20 columns of pure random noise to the developer data and refit:

                       7 real features  + 20 noise columns
    MLP (64, 32)                 0.434               0.483
    gradient boosting            0.429               0.430

The boosted trees hardly notice. The network’s test RMSE rises from 0.434 to 0.483: it spent part of its capacity fitting noise. Real datasets often have many weak or useless columns, and that is one reason trees remain the default for tabular economic data.

So where do networks win? On data where **single inputs mean little and structure is everything**: images, where one pixel tells you nothing and edges, strokes and shapes emerge from combinations; text, where meaning emerges from words in order; audio and video. There, networks with architecture built for the structure learn features that nobody could hand-craft.

Our digits are too small to show this at full strength: at 8 × 8 pixels, each pixel is nearly a feature on its own, and everything does well. But one test shows the difference in kind. Train each model on the digits as given, then test it on the same test digits shifted one pixel to the right, as if the scanner had slipped:

                       original test images  shifted one pixel right
    multinomial logit                 0.969                    0.417
    gradient boosting                 0.978                    0.444
    MLP (128)                         0.976                    0.470
    small CNN                         0.974                    0.606

On the original images the four models are within a couple of points of each other, and the boosted trees do best. Shift the images and the logit, the trees and the plain MLP collapse to 42%–47%, because each learned “pixel 27 is dark” rather than “there is a loop”. The **convolutional network** (CNN) keeps 61%. A convolution slides one small set of weights across the whole image, so a stroke detector learned in one place works in every place; pooling then summarises nearby positions. That built-in knowledge of space is the point of the architecture, and it is why networks dominate images and trees dominate spreadsheets.

### Worked example — choosing a model

For each task, would you start with gradient boosting or a neural network? **(a)** Predicting which small-business loans default, from 40 columns of firm and owner characteristics. **(b)** Estimating village poverty from satellite photographs. **(c)** Classifying a million job postings into occupations from their text.

### Solution

**(a)** Gradient boosting. It is a tabular problem with meaningful columns, some of them useless and some step-like (loan size bands, credit-score cut-offs), which is exactly where Grinsztajn et al. find trees ahead, and boosting needs little tuning. **(b)** A network, specifically a convolutional one, usually pre-trained on other images; the information is in spatial patterns of roofs, roads and fields that no spreadsheet column captures. This is the Jean et al. (2016) design in the readings. **(c)** A network-based language model (Chapters 20 and 21), or, as a strong cheap baseline, a linear model on word counts (Chapter 19). In practice the best answer for (b) and (c) is often a hybrid: let a network turn the image or text into a list of numbers, then feed those numbers to a simpler model, which is the idea of the next section.

## Embeddings: what the hidden layer learned

A trained network’s hidden layer is a **learned representation**: for each input, a list of numbers that the network built because they were useful for its task. For the digits MLP, each image becomes 128 hidden-unit outputs. Are those numbers a better description of a digit than its 64 raw pixels? Chapter 17 gives a way to ask: cluster the test images with k-means into ten groups, once on the pixels and once on the hidden layer, and compare each clustering with the true digits using the adjusted Rand index. On pixels it is 0.64; on the hidden layer, 0.93. The network has rearranged the images so that the same digit written differently lands in the same place.

Such a representation is called an **embedding**, and it is the bridge to Part IV. A network trained on billions of words learns an embedding for each word, a list of a few hundred numbers in which similar words sit close together, and an embedding for whole sentences and documents. Economists then use those numbers as features in an ordinary regression or a boosted tree, or measure distances between documents with them. Chapter 19 starts with word counts, the old way to turn text into numbers; Chapter 20 replaces them with embeddings; Chapter 21 looks inside the networks that make the best ones.

### Worked example — using an embedding

A researcher has 50,000 product descriptions from online listings and wants to measure how differentiated each product is from its competitors. How could a pre-trained network help, and what is the economist’s job?

### Solution

Pass each description through a pre-trained language network and keep its embedding, a few hundred numbers per product. Similar products now have nearby embeddings, so “differentiation” can be measured as the distance to the nearest competitors, and markets can be built by clustering (Chapter 17), much as Hoberg and Phillips did with word counts. The network does the reading. The economist decides what similarity should mean for the question, checks that distances in the embedding agree with judgement on a sample, and remembers that the embedding reflects the text the network was trained on, which may not be the text of this market.

## In Python

The whole tabular analysis, runnable top to bottom: data, a network in a pipeline with early stopping, and the comparison with ridge and boosting.

``` python
from pathlib import Path
import numpy as np, pandas as pd
from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split
from sklearn.compose import make_column_transformer
from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler, OneHotEncoder
from sklearn.pipeline import make_pipeline
from sklearn.neural_network import MLPRegressor
from sklearn.linear_model import RidgeCV
from sklearn.ensemble import HistGradientBoostingRegressor
from sklearn.metrics import root_mean_squared_error as rmse

BASE = "https://raw.githubusercontent.com/rfordatascience/tidytuesday/main/data/2024/2024-09-03/"
path = Path("../data/stackoverflow_survey_single_response.csv")     # download once, then reuse
if not path.exists():
    path.parent.mkdir(exist_ok=True)
    pd.read_csv(BASE + path.name).to_csv(path, index=False)
so = pd.read_csv(path)
```

Keep US professional developers paid in dollars, and predict log pay.

``` python
us = so[(so["country"] == "United States of America") & so["currency"].astype(str).str.startswith("USD")
        & so["comp_total"].between(1e4, 1e6) & (so["main_branch"] == 1)]
num, cat = ["years_code", "years_code_pro"], ["ed_level", "age", "dev_type", "org_size", "remote_work"]
df = us.dropna(subset=num + cat)
X, y = df[num + cat], np.log(df["comp_total"])
X_tr, X_te, y_tr, y_te = train_test_split(X, y, test_size=0.25, random_state=368)
```

Fit three models and score them once on the test set.

``` python
pre = make_column_transformer((StandardScaler(), num), (OneHotEncoder(handle_unknown="ignore"), cat))
models = {
    "ridge": make_pipeline(pre, RidgeCV()),
    "MLP": make_pipeline(pre, MLPRegressor(hidden_layer_sizes=(64, 32), alpha=1e-2, early_stopping=True,
                                           max_iter=300, random_state=368)),
    "boosting": HistGradientBoostingRegressor(categorical_features=cat, random_state=368)}
for name, m in models.items():
    print(f"{name:>9}: test RMSE {rmse(y_te, m.fit(X_tr, y_tr).predict(X_te)):.3f}")
```

        ridge: test RMSE 0.430
          MLP: test RMSE 0.434
     boosting: test RMSE 0.429

## What goes wrong

**Not scaling the inputs.** On raw columns the pay network’s test RMSE was 0.478, against 0.434 scaled. Standardize numbers, one-hot encode categories, and do both inside a pipeline so the scaler is fitted on training data only.

**Feeding category codes as numbers.** The survey stores job types as integers. A network multiplies inputs by weights, so it treats code 16 as sixteen times code 1. One-hot encode, or use an embedding layer for categories with many levels.

**Training without regularization.** A wide network on 1,000 developers had a training $R^2$ of 0.80 and a cross-validated $R^2$ of -0.08. Use early stopping always, and tune weight decay on a log grid.

**Reaching for a network on a spreadsheet.** On tabular data, gradient boosting usually matches or beats a network with less work, and it shrugs off useless columns that cost the network accuracy. Start with boosting; try a network if you have a reason.

**Reporting one run.** A network’s result depends on its random starting weights and the order of its mini-batches. Fix the seed for reproducibility, but before claiming one model beats another by a small margin, re-run with a few seeds.

**Reading the weights as effects.** The weights of a network have no individual meaning: shuffle the hidden units and you get the same predictions with different weights. And as with every model in Part III, a prediction that remote workers earn more is not evidence that remote work raises pay.

## Exercises

1.  A colleague says: “Deep learning is just a lot of logistic regressions.” In what sense is that right, and what does it leave out?

2.  Count by hand the number of weights and biases in an MLP with 10 inputs, hidden layers of 20 and 5 ReLU units, and one output. Check with scikit-learn’s `coefs_` and `intercepts_` after fitting on any data with 10 columns.

3.  Using the numpy network from the chapter, train the 8-unit network with learning rates 0.005, 0.05 and 0.5 for 3,000 steps each, and report the final loss. What happens when the learning rate is too large?

4.  Refit the pay MLP with three different `random_state` values and report the test RMSE of each. How large is the spread, compared with the gap between the MLP and gradient boosting?

5.  Add the 20 noise columns to the pay data and refit ridge regression. Does it degrade like the MLP or like the boosted trees? Why?

6.  Retrain the digits MLP with one-pixel shifts added to the training data (append copies of each training image moved one pixel left and one pixel right). How does it now do on the shifted test images, compared with the unaugmented MLP and the CNN? What does that tell you about where the CNN’s advantage comes from?

7.  Your best pay model predicts that, holding the other features fixed, developers who work remotely are paid more. Give two reasons this is not the causal effect of remote work on pay.

### Answers

**1.** Right in that each unit is a weighted sum passed through a nonlinear curve, and a sigmoid unit is literally a logistic regression; a network’s softmax output layer is a multinomial logit on the last hidden layer. What it leaves out: the hidden units are not fitted to any label of their own. They are trained jointly, through backpropagation, to produce whatever features make the final prediction best, and the architecture (convolutions for images, attention for text) builds in structure that no stack of separate logits has.

**2.** From 10 inputs to 20 units: 10 × 20 + 20 = 220. From 20 to 5: 20 × 5 + 5 = 105. From 5 to the output: 5 + 1 = 6. Total 331.

``` python
Xr = np.random.default_rng(368).normal(size=(200, 10))
m2 = MLPRegressor(hidden_layer_sizes=(20, 5), max_iter=50, random_state=368).fit(Xr, Xr[:, 0])
print(sum(w.size for w in m2.coefs_) + sum(b.size for b in m2.intercepts_))
```

    331

**3.**

``` python
final = {}
for eta in [0.005, 0.05, 0.5]:
    prm = init(1, 8, np.random.default_rng(368))
    for step in range(3000):
        loss, g = forward_backward(prm, xs, ys)
        prm = {k: prm[k] - eta * g[k] for k in prm}
    final[eta] = loss
print({k: float(np.round(v, 4)) for k, v in final.items()})
```

    {0.005: 0.1056, 0.05: 0.1043, 0.5: 0.1145}

The final losses are 0.1056, 0.1043, 0.1145 for learning rates 0.005, 0.05 and 0.5. With 0.5 the loss ends higher than with 0.05: the steps are so large that they bounce around the floor of the valley instead of settling in it, and on a harder problem steps this large typically overshoot and the loss blows up. With 0.005 the network is still crawling downhill after 3,000 steps. The learning rate is the first setting to tune.

**4.**

``` python
seed_rmse = {s: rmse(y_te, make_pipeline(make_pre(), MLPRegressor(**{**MLP_ARGS, "random_state": s}))
                     .fit(X_tr, y_tr).predict(X_te)) for s in [1, 2, 3]}
print({s: round(v, 4) for s, v in seed_rmse.items()})
```

    {1: 0.438, 2: 0.4347, 3: 0.4279}

The test RMSEs range over 0.010 log points across seeds, against a gap of 0.005 between the chapter’s MLP and gradient boosting. The seed matters about as much as the choice of model, so a single run cannot settle which is better.

**5.**

``` python
ridge_n = make_pipeline(make_pre(num + list(noise.columns)), RidgeCV()).fit(Xn_tr, y_tr)
print(round(res["ridge (one-hot)"], 4), round(rmse(y_te, ridge_n.predict(Xn_te)), 4))
```

    0.4304 0.431

Ridge goes from 0.430 to 0.431: it barely degrades, like the trees. With 4,733 training rows and only 20 extra coefficients, each shrunk by the penalty, a linear model estimates the useless coefficients as nearly zero. The network suffers more because every noise column feeds 64 hidden units, so it adds over a thousand weights that can combine with each other to fit noise.

**6.**

``` python
def shift_any(Xflat, dx):
    im = Xflat.reshape(-1, 8, 8)
    return np.roll(im, dx, axis=2).reshape(-1, 64)        # wrap-around shift; edges are mostly blank
Xaug = np.vstack([Xd_tr, shift_any(Xd_tr, 1), shift_any(Xd_tr, -1)])
yaug = np.concatenate([yd_tr] * 3)
mlp_aug = MLPClassifier(hidden_layer_sizes=(128,), alpha=1e-3, max_iter=500, random_state=368).fit(Xaug, yaug)
print(round(mlp_aug.score(Xd_te, yd_te), 3), round(mlp_aug.score(Xs_te, yd_te), 3))
```

    0.978 0.974

Trained on shifted copies (**data augmentation**), the plain MLP gets 97% of the shifted test digits right, against 47% without augmentation and 61% for the unaugmented CNN. So the CNN’s advantage here can be bought, and more, by showing an ordinary network examples of shifted digits. The CNN gets robustness to shifts for free from its architecture; augmentation gets it only for the variations you thought to include. With real images, which vary in position, scale, angle and lighting all at once, building the structure in is far cheaper than enumerating every variation, which is why image models combine both.

**7.** First, **selection**: who gets to work remotely is not random. Remote arrangements are more common for experienced, trusted developers and at well-paying technology firms, and the survey does not record employer, city or skill, all of which raise pay and remote work together. Second, **reverse causation and bargaining**: highly paid developers have the leverage to negotiate remote work, so pay may cause remoteness rather than the reverse. A predictive model is built to exploit such correlations, not to separate them; a causal answer needs a design, such as a firm that randomised remote work (Chapter 7).

## Economics reading

**Jean, N., Burke, M., Xie, M., Davis, W. M., Lobell, D. B. & Ermon, S. (2016). “Combining satellite imagery and machine learning to predict poverty.” *Science* 353(6301): 790–794.** [doi.org/10.1126/science.aaf7894](https://doi.org/10.1126/science.aaf7894)

Household consumption surveys are expensive and rare in much of Africa, so poverty maps are often years out of date. The authors train a convolutional network to predict night-time lights, which are measured everywhere, from daytime satellite photographs, then reuse the features it learned to predict survey-measured consumption and assets in five African countries. The features capture roads, roofs and farmland, and predict poverty far better than night-time lights alone.

*Think about:* the network is trained to predict lights, not poverty. What would go wrong if a government used its poverty predictions to target cash transfers, and villages learned what the satellite rewards?

**Dell, M. (2025). “Deep Learning for Economists.” *Journal of Economic Literature* 63(1): 5–58.** [doi.org/10.1257/jel.20241733](https://doi.org/10.1257/jel.20241733)

A survey and practical guide to deep learning for economic research, written by an economic historian. It explains how networks turn documents, images and text into data: digitising historical newspapers and firm directories, classifying text and images at scale, and linking records across sources, with code and tools aimed at economists.

*Think about:* when a network creates a dataset (say, firm names read off scanned 1920s directories), its errors are not random. How could systematic misreadings bias an economic conclusion built on the new data, and how would you check?

**Farrell, M. H., Liang, T. & Misra, S. (2021). “Deep Neural Networks for Estimation and Inference.” *Econometrica* 89(1): 181–213.** [doi.org/10.3982/ecta16901](https://doi.org/10.3982/ecta16901)

Can a flexible network be used inside an econometric estimator without ruining the standard errors? The authors show that networks learn unknown functions fast enough to serve as the first stage of semiparametric estimators, such as estimates of average treatment effects that adjust for many covariates, and apply the method to data from a large-scale marketing experiment.

*Think about:* this chapter warned that a network’s weights are not causal effects. In what sense can a network still be part of a credible causal estimate, and which part of the analysis must still come from the research design rather than the network?

**Reading.** Géron, ch. 9–11, for neural network basics, building networks with PyTorch, and training deep networks (initialisation, optimisers, batch normalisation, dropout). ISLP (James, Witten, Hastie, Tibshirani & Taylor 2023), ch. 10, for deep learning in a statistical framing. Goodfellow, Bengio & Courville (2016), *Deep Learning*, ch. 6, free online. Grinsztajn, Oyallon & Varoquaux (2022), “Why Do Tree-Based Models Still Outperform Deep Learning on Typical Tabular Data?”, *NeurIPS* 35, for the trees-versus-nets benchmark.



# Chapter 19 — Text as data

> **The question:** central banks, landlords and newspapers all publish words, not numbers. How do you turn those words into a variable you can put in a regression, and how do you know the variable measures what you claim it measures?

## Words are data

Most of what happens in an economy is written down before it is counted. The Federal Open Market Committee (FOMC) releases a statement after every meeting, minutes three weeks later, and transcripts five years later. Firms file 10-Ks and hold earnings calls. Every rental flat in San Francisco was, for most of the 2000s, advertised in a one-line Craigslist title. Newspapers report on all of it. None of this arrives as a column in a spreadsheet.

Economists used to read such text and code it by hand. That still happens, and it is still the gold standard, but it does not scale: you can read 500 FOMC sentences in an afternoon, not 5 million newspaper articles. **Text as data** is the set of methods for doing the reading by machine.

Gentzkow, Kelly and Taddy (2019) describe every text project with the same three steps, and I find their notation worth learning because it makes you say what you are doing:

1.  **Represent.** Turn the raw documents $\mathcal{D}$ into a numerical array $\mathbf{C}$. Usually $\mathbf{C}$ is a **document-term matrix**: one row per document, one column per word, each cell a count.
2.  **Measure.** Map $\mathbf{C}$ to an estimate $\hat{V}$ of the thing you care about but cannot observe: the tone of a statement, how uncertain the newspapers sound, what a listing says about a flat.
3.  **Use.** Put $\hat V$ into ordinary economic analysis: a regression, a forecast, an event study.

There are four broad ways to do step 2. A **dictionary** method counts words from a list someone wrote down. A **supervised** method learns which words matter from documents that humans have already labelled. A **topic model** finds groups of words that travel together, with no labels at all. And **embeddings** and **language models** replace word counts with dense learned vectors. This chapter does the first two. Chapter 20 does topics and embeddings, and Chapter 21 does transformers and large language models.

The thing to hold on to throughout is that $\hat V$ is a **measurement**. It has error, and its error may be correlated with the things you later put on the right-hand side. Most of the hard thinking in a text project goes into convincing yourself, and a referee, that $\hat V$ measures $V$.

### Worked example — naming the pieces

For each project, name $\mathcal{D}$, $\hat V$, and whether the measurement step is a dictionary or supervised. **(a)** Baker, Bloom and Davis (2016) count newspaper articles that mention the economy, policy and uncertainty. **(b)** A researcher has 2,000 FOMC sentences that economists have labelled hawkish or dovish, and wants to score the other 50,000. **(c)** A housing office wants to know how much the words in a rental ad add to the predicted rent.

### Solution

**(a)** $\mathcal{D}$ is newspaper articles; $\hat V$ is the share of articles each month about economic policy uncertainty; the method is a dictionary, three short word lists chosen by the authors. **(b)** $\mathcal{D}$ is FOMC sentences; $\hat V$ is each sentence’s stance; supervised, because the labelled sentences teach the model which words signal hawkishness. **(c)** $\mathcal{D}$ is ad titles; $\hat V$ is the predicted (log) rent; supervised, with the observed rent as the label. Notice that in (c) the label is a number we already have for every ad. We are not trying to measure rent, which we observe; we are asking what the text knows about rent beyond the structured columns.

## Regular expressions: cleaning before counting

Before any counting, text usually needs cleaning, and the tool for that is the **regular expression**, or regex: a small language for describing patterns in strings. You have already met pandas’ `.str` methods; regex is what goes inside them.

Five pieces cover most of what an economist needs. `\d` is a digit, `\w` a letter, digit or underscore, `\s` a space. `+` means “one or more”, `*` “zero or more”, `?` “optional”. Square brackets give a set, so `[Ss]ales` matches both capitalisations. `\b` is a word boundary, so `\bfed\b` matches “fed” but not “federal”. Parentheses capture a piece you want to keep. Always write patterns as raw strings, `r"..."`, so Python does not eat the backslashes, and test them at regex101.com before trusting them.

Here is a sentence from a 2023 FOMC statement, and a pattern that pulls out the target range for the federal funds rate:

``` python
stmt = ("The Committee decided to maintain the target range for the federal funds "
        "rate at 5-1/4 to 5-1/2 percent.")
m = re.search(r"(\d+)-(\d)/(\d) to (\d+)-(\d)/(\d) percent", stmt)
lo = int(m[1]) + int(m[2]) / int(m[3])
hi = int(m[4]) + int(m[5]) / int(m[6])
print(f"target range: {lo:.2f}% to {hi:.2f}%")
```

    target range: 5.25% to 5.50%

Our main dataset in this chapter is 200,796 Craigslist rental ads from the Bay Area, 2000 to 2018, collected by Kate Pennington from archived pages and distributed by TidyTuesday; it is the same file we used in Chapter 9. Craigslist generated the start of many titles automatically, like `$1895 / 2br - 850ft² - THIS 2BR APT. IS THE ONE!`. One regex with named groups pulls the three pieces out:

``` python
pat = r"^\$(?P<t_price>\d+)\s*(?:/\s*(?P<t_beds>\d+)br)?\s*-\s*(?:(?P<t_sqft>\d+)ft²\s*-\s*)?"
ex = rent["title"].str.extract(pat).astype(float)
ex.notna().mean().round(3)
```

    t_price    0.708
    t_beds     0.656
    t_sqft     0.133
    dtype: float64

So 71% of titles start with a price, 66% with a bedroom count, and 13% with square feet.

### Worked example — checking a regex against something you know

How would you know the regex is right? Compare what it extracts with columns you already have.

### Solution

Where both exist, the price in the title equals the `price` column in 99.99% of ads, and the bedroom count equals `beds` in 99.998%. That is the check: a regex is a measurement instrument, and you validate an instrument on cases where you know the answer. The 20 disagreements are worth reading. Several are titles like `$12951395 / 2br`, where a landlord typed a range, \$1,295 to \$1,395, and the dash got lost. Those are not regex errors; they are data errors that the regex revealed.

The same check delivers a warning we will need later. If the title literally contains the price in most ads, then any model that “predicts rent from the text of the title” is going to read the answer off the page. Hold that thought.

## From strings to tokens

The second dataset in this chapter is a set of sentences from FOMC statements, minutes and speeches between 1996 and 2022, which Shah, Paturi and Chava (2023) hand-labelled as **hawkish** (leaning towards tighter policy), **dovish** (leaning towards easier policy) or **neutral**. There are 1,984 sentences in their training file and 496 in their test file. Here are three, with their human labels:

    [hawkish] Inflation was still expected to be somewhat higher this year than last year, largely reflecting an upturn in the prices for food and non-energy imports.
    [neutral] Moreover, to the extent that more-rapid growth of productivity shows through to faster gains in nominal wages, there will be fewer instances in which nominal wages will be pressured to fall.
    [neutral] in turn, larger expected productivity advances and a lower cost of equity capital provided a further stimulus to investment.

To count words, we first have to decide what a word is. Splitting text into units is called **tokenization**, and the simplest useful tokenizer is a regex: `re.findall(r"[a-z]+", text.lower())` lowercases the text and keeps runs of letters. Every such choice throws something away. Lowercasing merges “Fed” and “fed”; dropping digits throws away “2%”; dropping punctuation turns “don’t” into “don” and “t”. Whether that matters depends on what $V$ is.

Count tokens across all 2,480 FOMC sentences and the most common are not exciting: the, of, in, and, to, that. These are **stopwords**, the grammatical glue of English; together with fragments of one or two letters they make up 42% of all tokens. Drop them using NLTK’s list and the top words become inflation, policy, growth, monetary, economic, rate. That is what FOMC sentences are about.

Stopwords are not always noise. “Not”, “no” and “but” carry most of the meaning in “inflation has not declined”. Pronouns tell you whether a politician says “I” or “we”. Drop stopwords when you want topics; think twice when you want tone.

The next step is optional. **Stemming** chops words down to a common root with crude rules, so “employment”, “employed” and “employs” all become “employ”; the Porter stemmer is the classic. **Lemmatization** looks words up in a dictionary to find their base form, so “better” becomes “good”. Both shrink the vocabulary, which helps when documents are short and data are scarce, at the cost of merging words that mean different things.

``` python
words = ["economies", "economic", "employment", "employed", "inflationary", "rates", "uncertainty"]
print([porter.stem(w) for w in words])
```

    ['economi', 'econom', 'employ', 'employ', 'inflationari', 'rate', 'uncertainti']

Finally, single words lose order. “Interest” and “rates” as separate tokens are not quite “interest rates”, and “not” next to “accommodative” means something different from “not” anywhere in the sentence. An **n-gram** is a run of $n$ consecutive tokens: bigrams are pairs, trigrams are triples. Adding bigrams keeps a little word order, and the price is a vocabulary that grows very fast.

### Worked example — how much does stemming shrink the vocabulary?

The FOMC sentences contain 4,595 distinct content words. Predict roughly how many distinct Porter stems they collapse to, then check.

### Solution

They collapse to 2,904 stems, a cut of 37%. Each stem stands for one or two surface forms on average, which is about what you would expect from English inflection (“rate”, “rates”, “rated”). Whether that is a good trade depends on the task. For 2,000 short sentences, merging “tighten”, “tightened” and “tightening” pools evidence that would otherwise be spread thin. For a task where tense matters (“rates rose” against “rates will rise”) it destroys exactly what you need.

## Bag of words and tf-idf

The **bag-of-words** representation throws away order altogether: each document becomes a row of counts, one column per word in the vocabulary. scikit-learn’s `CountVectorizer` builds the whole document-term matrix in one line:

``` python
cv = CountVectorizer(stop_words="english", min_df=3)    # keep words in >= 3 sentences
C = cv.fit_transform(fomc_all["sentence"])
print(C.shape, f"share of zeros: {1 - C.nnz / np.prod(C.shape):.1%}")
```

    (2480, 2052) share of zeros: 99.3%

That is 2,480 sentences by 2,052 words, and 99.3% of the cells are zero, because any one sentence uses only a handful of the words. scikit-learn stores it as a **sparse** matrix, keeping only the nonzero entries. Never call `.toarray()` on a real corpus: a million documents by fifty thousand words is fifty billion numbers.

`min_df=3` drops words that appear in fewer than three documents. Rare words are mostly typos and names, and a word that appears once cannot teach a model anything it can use on new text. `max_df` does the reverse, dropping words that appear almost everywhere.

Raw counts have a problem: they are dominated by words that every document uses. In FOMC text, “committee” and “inflation” appear all over the place and so say little about any particular sentence. **tf-idf** (term frequency, inverse document frequency) reweights each count by how rare the word is across documents:

$$\text{tf-idf}_{ij} = c_{ij} \times \left(\log\frac{1 + n}{1 + \text{df}_j} + 1\right)$$

where $c_{ij}$ is the count of word $j$ in document $i$, $n$ is the number of documents, and $\text{df}_j$ is the number of documents containing word $j$. A word in every document gets the minimum weight; a word in very few gets a large one. That formula is scikit-learn’s default; each row is then scaled to length one, so long documents do not dominate. Setting `sublinear_tf=True` replaces the raw count by $1 + \log c_{ij}$, so a word used ten times is not ten times as important as a word used once.

### Worked example — tf-idf by hand

Take three made-up documents: “rates rose”, “rates fell”, “wheat rose”. Compute the tf-idf weights of “rates” and “wheat” in the documents that contain them, before the rows are scaled to length one. Then check against scikit-learn.

### Solution

There are $n = 3$ documents. “rates” appears in 2, so its idf is $\log(4/3) + 1 \approx 1.29$. “wheat” appears in 1, so its idf is $\log(4/2) + 1 \approx 1.69$. Each appears once in its documents, so those are the weights. “wheat” gets more weight because it is rarer: it does more to tell the third document apart from the others.

``` python
toy = TfidfVectorizer(norm=None).fit(["rates rose", "rates fell", "wheat rose"])
print({w: round(float(v), 3) for w, v in zip(toy.get_feature_names_out(), toy.idf_)})
```

    {'fell': 1.693, 'rates': 1.288, 'rose': 1.288, 'wheat': 1.693}

scikit-learn agrees. The bag of words also shows its limits here: “rates rose” and “rates fell” share half their words, and nothing in the matrix knows that “rose” and “fell” are opposites. Chapter 20’s embeddings are the first step towards fixing that, and they will turn out to have their own trouble with exactly this pair.

## Dictionary methods

The oldest way from $\mathbf{C}$ to $\hat V$ is a **dictionary**: write down a list of words that signal the concept, and score each document by the share of its words that are on the list,

$$\hat V_i = \frac{\text{number of dictionary words in document } i}{\text{number of words in document } i}.$$

Dictionaries are transparent (a referee can read the list), they need no labelled data, and they run on a billion documents as easily as on ten. Their weaknesses are just as plain. They are blind to context, so “not good” counts as good. And a word list written for one domain can be badly wrong in another.

That second point is the lesson of Loughran and McDonald (2011). Finance researchers had been measuring the tone of 10-K filings with a general-purpose list of negative words from psychology. Loughran and McDonald showed that most of the words on it are not negative in a 10-K at all: “tax”, “cost”, “liability” and “capital” are ordinary accounting vocabulary, “crude” is a kind of oil, “mine” is a kind of company. They built finance-specific lists (negative, positive, uncertainty, litigious and others) from the 10-Ks themselves, and those lists track returns and volatility much better. The full Loughran–McDonald dictionary is free from Notre Dame’s Software Repository for Accounting and Finance.

Here I use something much smaller: three **illustrative** word lists of ten to thirteen words each, in the spirit of their categories but written for this chapter. They are not the Loughran–McDonald dictionary and should not be cited as such. The point is the mechanics.

``` python
print(sorted(lm["negative"]))
def lm_scores(text):
    toks = tokens(text); n = max(len(toks), 1)
    out = {k: 100 * sum(t in v for t in toks) / n for k, v in lm.items()}
    out["net_tone"] = out["positive"] - out["negative"]      # per 100 words
    return out
lm_scores("Inflation has not declined; the outlook is not favorable.")
```

    ['adverse', 'decline', 'declined', 'deteriorated', 'difficult', 'downturn', 'loss', 'losses', 'slowed', 'stress', 'unemployment', 'weak', 'weakened']

    {'negative': 11.11111111111111,
     'positive': 11.11111111111111,
     'uncertainty': 0.0,
     'net_tone': 0.0}

That last sentence shows the classic failure. “Not declined” counts as negative and “not favorable” as positive, so the score is zero for a sentence that is plainly worried.

### Worked example — does a tone dictionary measure stance?

A tempting shortcut: a positive tone means a strong economy, a strong economy means the Fed leans hawkish, so net tone should measure hawkishness. Score the 1,984 labelled training sentences and check.

### Solution

The average net tone, in dictionary words per 100 words, is:

    stance
    dovish    -0.47
    hawkish    0.49
    neutral   -0.11

The direction is right: hawkish sentences sound more positive than dovish ones. But the dictionary scores 73% of sentences exactly zero, because they contain none of the words. As a classifier of hawkish against dovish, net tone has an AUC (Chapter 12) of 0.60, where 0.5 is a coin flip. That is weak, and the reason is economic, not computational: tone and stance are different concepts. “Inflation pressures have increased” uses a positive word for a hawkish worry; “unemployment remains elevated” is dovish without being negative in any obvious way. A dictionary measures what its words measure. If the concept you want is stance, either write a stance dictionary or, better, learn one from labels, which is the next section.

### The Economic Policy Uncertainty index

The most successful dictionary in economics is Baker, Bloom and Davis’s (2016) **Economic Policy Uncertainty** (EPU) index. The logic is a three-way filter. An article counts if it contains at least one term from *each* of three lists:

- **E**conomy: “economic”, “economy”;
- **P**olicy: “Congress”, “deficit”, “Federal Reserve”, “legislation”, “regulation”, “White House”;
- **U**ncertainty: “uncertain”, “uncertainty”.

Each paper’s monthly count is divided by its total number of articles, standardised, averaged across ten major US newspapers, and scaled so that the index averaged 100 over 1985–2009. Requiring all three sets is what makes it precise: “uncertainty” alone catches sports and weather, “economy” alone catches everything. As regexes:

``` python
E = re.compile(r"\beconom(?:y|ic)\b", re.I)
P = re.compile(r"\b(?:congress|deficit|federal reserve|legislation|regulation|white house)\b", re.I)
U = re.compile(r"\buncertain(?:ty)?\b", re.I)
def is_epu(article):
    return all(rx.search(article) for rx in (E, P, U))
[is_epu(h) for h in ["Uncertainty over White House tariff plans weighs on the economy",
                     "Economic growth beats forecasts as consumers keep spending",
                     "Federal Reserve holds rates steady"]]
```

    [True, False, False]

Only the first headline passes all three. We cannot run the filter on ten newspapers ourselves, but FRED publishes the result as series `USEPUINDXM`, and anyone can download it without a key:

![The US Economic Policy Uncertainty index, monthly, from FRED (series USEPUINDXM).](19_text_as_data_files/figure-commonmark/cell-17-output-1.png)

The series runs from 1985 to September 2026. Its 1985–2009 average is 101.1, close to the 100 the construction promises (the published series has been revised since it was first scaled). It reached 190 in October 2008 and 350 during the 2020 pandemic, and its highest value so far, 460, came in April 2025, the month of the broad US tariff announcements.

Ten words, applied to millions of articles, gave macroeconomists a monthly measure of something they had theorised about for decades and never observed. Baker, Bloom and Davis then show that firms more exposed to government policy cut investment and hiring when the index rises. Just as important is how they validated it: research assistants read and coded thousands of articles by hand, and the dictionary index tracks the human one closely. A dictionary you have not checked against human judgement is a guess.

### Worked example — what would break the EPU filter?

Suppose a newspaper changes its style guide and starts writing “the Fed” instead of “the Federal Reserve”. What happens to that paper’s contribution to the index, and how would you notice?

### Solution

Articles about Fed policy uncertainty that used to pass the P filter now fail it, unless they also mention Congress, the deficit or another policy term, so the paper’s EPU count falls for reasons that have nothing to do with uncertainty. Because each paper is standardised separately, the drop shows up as a level shift in one paper’s series, starting on the date of the style change. You would notice it by plotting each newspaper’s series separately and looking for breaks that the other papers do not share. This is the general weakness of dictionaries: their validity rests on the words, and the words people use change over time even when the world does not.

## Supervised text regression

If someone has already labelled some documents, you do not have to guess the dictionary. Let the labels choose it. Put the tf-idf matrix on the right-hand side of a regression (logistic for a category, linear for a number) and let the data decide which words predict the label.

The obstacle is obvious to anyone who has run a regression. Text is **high-dimensional**: with unigrams and bigrams, our FOMC training sentences have thousands of columns and fewer than a thousand hawkish or dovish rows. OLS cannot even be computed when there are more columns than rows. Chapter 11 has the answer. The **LASSO** adds a penalty $\lambda \sum_j |\beta_j|$ to the loss, which sets most coefficients to exactly zero; cross-validation picks $\lambda$. In this setting the LASSO has a lovely interpretation: the words it keeps, with their signs, are a **learned dictionary**.

Keep the hawkish and dovish sentences (dropping neutral gives a clean yes/no problem), and fit:

``` python
from sklearn.linear_model import LogisticRegressionCV
from sklearn.pipeline import make_pipeline
bin_tr, bin_te = train[train["label"] < 2], test[test["label"] < 2]    # 1 = hawkish, 0 = dovish
model = make_pipeline(
    TfidfVectorizer(ngram_range=(1, 2), min_df=2, sublinear_tf=True),
    LogisticRegressionCV(Cs=10, cv=5, penalty="l1", solver="liblinear",
                         scoring="accuracy", random_state=368))
model.fit(bin_tr["sentence"], bin_tr["label"]);
```

On the 249 held-out test sentences, the model is right 73.1% of the time, against 54.2% for always guessing the more common class. Cross-validation kept 188 of 5,599 features. Here are the ones with the largest weights:

![The learned dictionary: the largest LASSO coefficients for hawkish (garnet) and dovish (grey).](19_text_as_data_files/figure-commonmark/cell-20-output-1.png)

The hawkish side has promote, higher, inflation, up, stability, solid; the dovish side has subdued, lower, productivity, downside, weaker, accommodative. Much of that is central-bank vocabulary that an economist would have written down: inflation and higher on one side, subdued, lower, downside and accommodative on the other. Nobody told the model. Some of it is economics an economist might not have thought to write down: “productivity” leans dovish, presumably because faster productivity growth lets the economy grow without inflation, which argues for patience. And some of it no economist would choose, and those are the words to stare at. A word like “promote” probably reflects which documents happened to be sampled and labelled (speeches that “promote” price stability, say), not anything about the stance of policy. A learned dictionary is only as general as the sample that taught it.

### Worked example — when the text contains the label

Now the rental ads. Restrict to San Francisco, rents between \$500 and \$10,000, and known bedroom counts, which leaves 52,798 ads; split them 75/25. A colleague fits a LASSO of log rent on the tf-idf of the raw, lowercased titles and reports a test $R^2$ of 0.79. Is the text really that informative?

### Solution

No. 87% of all titles contain a dollar figure, and we saw that it matches the rent almost perfectly. The model has learned to read “\$2400” as a feature. It is the text version of Chapter 12’s `reservation_status`: a feature that contains the outcome. Exercise 22’s fix is to remove every dollar amount, every “Nbr” and every square-footage pattern anywhere in the title, not only in the prefix:

``` python
LEAK = r"\$\s?\d[\d,]*|\b\d+\s?br\b|\b\d+\s?ft(?:²|2)"
rent["text"] = (rent["title"].fillna("").map(html.unescape).str.lower()
                .str.replace(LEAK, " ", regex=True)
                .str.replace(r"\s+", " ", regex=True).str.strip(" -/"))
assert not rent["text"].str.contains(r"\$\d").any()
```

Refit on the cleaned titles, with the penalty chosen by three-fold cross-validation, and the test $R^2$ falls to 0.52. That is the honest number, and it is still respectable: words like “studio”, “room” and “furnished” really do tell you about a flat. The gap of 0.27 was the price itself.

### What the rental LASSO learned

The cleaned model keeps 1,522 of 5,038 features. Its largest positive and negative coefficients:

    higher rent: resim, 图片, seasons, wavro, 3456, 3brm, 3ba, regis, sleek, paragon, rmdld view, skybox
    lower rent:  studio, share, room available, remodeled pet, studios, law, room rent, neighbrs, bay area, francisco ca, roomate, efficiency

The negative side is mostly sensible: rooms, shares, studios, roommates. The positive side is a lesson in reading a learned dictionary. Some of it is informative (luxury buildings, garages, views). Some of it is **leakage that my regex missed**: “3brm” is a bedroom count written in a form the regex did not anticipate, and “3ba” and “4ba” are bathroom counts, which mark big flats; 9.0% of all the cleaned titles still contain a bathroom count. Some of it is a **proxy for time**: “resim” (Turkish for “picture”) and 图片 (Chinese for “picture”) are words that appeared in archived Craigslist pages in later years, when rents were higher, so the model uses them as a calendar. And some features are the names of realtors or buildings that post many listings, which identify a landlord rather than describe a flat.

None of these would show up in a test $R^2$. They all show up in five minutes of reading coefficients.

## Measurement is a research design

Everything in this chapter produces a $\hat V$ that is meant to go into something else: an index into a VAR, a stance score into an event study, a text-predicted rent into a housing study. So the usual econometric worries apply, with a twist. $\hat V$ is a **generated regressor** measured with error, and the errors are not always noise.

If the error is pure noise, unrelated to everything else, a $\hat V$ on the right-hand side biases its coefficient towards zero (attenuation), and a $\hat V$ on the left-hand side just widens your standard errors. That is the friendly case. The unfriendly case is error that is correlated with the thing you are studying. If the FOMC changed its vocabulary after 2008, and the classifier was trained mostly on pre-2008 sentences, then its errors are correlated with time, and any “change in stance after 2008” is partly a change in vocabulary. If a newspaper’s style guide changes, the EPU index jumps.

Three habits help. **Validate against humans**: hand-label a few hundred documents the model has not seen and report agreement, as Baker, Bloom and Davis did. **Look at the errors**, not just the accuracy: read the documents the model gets wrong, and check whether errors cluster by year, source or group. **Report robustness to preprocessing**: Denny and Spirling (2018) show that results can flip with choices as innocent-looking as stopword lists and stemming. Treat the pipeline the way you treat a set of control variables: show the result under a few reasonable alternatives.

### Worked example — a noisy stance index

You want to use the LASSO’s predictions to compare how hawkish the Fed sounded before and after 2008. Compare the model’s predicted hawkish share with the humans’ share in each period, on the test sentences.

### Solution

                    sentences  accuracy  human hawkish  predicted hawkish
    era                                                                  
    2008 and after      133.0     0.729          0.436              0.481
    before 2008         116.0     0.733          0.483              0.509

Read the last two columns as the measurement check. If the predicted and human shares move together across the two periods, the classifier preserves the comparison you care about, even though it misclassifies individual sentences. If accuracy differs a lot between the periods, the errors are correlated with time and your before-and-after comparison is partly an artefact. With only 116 to 133 test sentences per period, differences of a few percentage points are well within sampling noise (Chapter 8’s bootstrap would put a number on that), so the honest conclusion from a table like this is usually “no evidence of drift, and not much power to detect it”. That is worth saying out loud in a paper.

## In Python

The two supervised pipelines from start to finish: the FOMC stance classifier and the rental-title regression.

``` python
import re, html
from pathlib import Path
import numpy as np, pandas as pd
from sklearn.feature_extraction.text import TfidfVectorizer
from sklearn.linear_model import LogisticRegressionCV, LassoCV
from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split
from sklearn.pipeline import make_pipeline
from sklearn.metrics import accuracy_score, r2_score
SEED = 368
```

Load the FOMC sentences once and cache them; Shah, Paturi and Chava’s own train/test split is the one to use, so results are comparable with theirs.

``` python
HF = "https://huggingface.co/datasets/gtfintechlab/fomc_communication/resolve/main/"
def cached_csv(name, url):
    path = Path("../data") / name
    if not path.exists():
        pd.read_csv(url).to_csv(path, index=False)
    return pd.read_csv(path)
train, test = cached_csv("fomc_train.csv", HF + "train.csv"), cached_csv("fomc_test.csv", HF + "test.csv")
for df_ in (train, test):
    df_["stance"] = df_["label"].map({0: "dovish", 1: "hawkish", 2: "neutral"})
bin_tr, bin_te = train[train["label"] < 2], test[test["label"] < 2]
```

Fit the LASSO logistic regression on tf-idf, then score once on the test set.

``` python
stance = make_pipeline(TfidfVectorizer(ngram_range=(1, 2), min_df=2, sublinear_tf=True),
                       LogisticRegressionCV(Cs=10, cv=5, penalty="l1", solver="liblinear",
                                            scoring="accuracy", random_state=SEED))
stance.fit(bin_tr["sentence"], bin_tr["label"])
print(f"test accuracy {accuracy_score(bin_te['label'], stance.predict(bin_te['sentence'])):.3f}, "
      f"majority class {bin_te['label'].value_counts(normalize=True).max():.3f}")
```

    test accuracy 0.731, majority class 0.542

The rental titles: clean out the leak, restrict to San Francisco, split.

``` python
rent = cached_csv("rent.csv", RENT_URL)
rent["log_price"] = np.log(rent["price"])
LEAK = r"\$\s?\d[\d,]*|\b\d+\s?br\b|\b\d+\s?ft(?:²|2)"
rent["text"] = (rent["title"].fillna("").map(html.unescape).str.lower()
                .str.replace(LEAK, " ", regex=True).str.replace(r"\s+", " ", regex=True).str.strip(" -/"))
sf = (rent.query("city == 'san francisco' and 500 <= price <= 10000")
          .dropna(subset=["beds"]).loc[lambda d: d["text"].str.len() > 0])
tr, te = train_test_split(sf, test_size=0.25, random_state=SEED)
```

Text alone, then structure alone (bedrooms, year and neighbourhood dummies), then both.

``` python
import scipy.sparse as sp
from sklearn.linear_model import LinearRegression, Lasso
tfv = TfidfVectorizer(min_df=10, ngram_range=(1, 2), sublinear_tf=True, stop_words="english")
T_tr, T_te = tfv.fit_transform(tr["text"]), tfv.transform(te["text"])
ybar = tr["log_price"].mean()
text_only = LassoCV(cv=3, alphas=np.logspace(-4.5, -2.5, 5), max_iter=3000).fit(T_tr, tr["log_price"] - ybar)
dummies = lambda d: pd.get_dummies(d[["nhood", "year"]].astype(str)).assign(beds=d["beds"]).astype(float)
S_tr = dummies(tr); S_te = dummies(te).reindex(columns=S_tr.columns, fill_value=0)
struct = LinearRegression().fit(S_tr, tr["log_price"])
both = Lasso(alpha=text_only.alpha_, max_iter=3000).fit(
    sp.hstack([T_tr, sp.csr_matrix(S_tr.values)]).tocsr(), tr["log_price"] - ybar)
```

``` python
r2 = {"text only": r2_score(te["log_price"], text_only.predict(T_te) + ybar),
      "structure only": r2_score(te["log_price"], struct.predict(S_te)),
      "text + structure": r2_score(te["log_price"],
                                   both.predict(sp.hstack([T_te, sp.csr_matrix(S_te.values)]).tocsr()) + ybar)}
pd.Series(r2).round(3)
```

    text only           0.517
    structure only      0.679
    text + structure    0.772
    dtype: float64

Bedrooms, neighbourhood and year explain 68% of the variance in log rent on the test ads; the cleaned title alone explains 52%; together they explain 77%. So the title adds 9 percentage points beyond the structured columns. Some of that is real information about the flat and some, as we saw, is bathrooms and calendar years sneaking in through the words. (One technical note: the “both” model reuses the penalty chosen for text alone and leaves the dummies unscaled, so it is a quick comparison, not a tuned model.)

## What goes wrong

**Leaving the label in the text.** 87% of rental titles contain the price, and a model trained on raw titles scores an $R^2$ of 0.79 by reading it. Ask of every text field what it records and when: a title written by the landlord after choosing the rent can contain the rent. The same goes for coders’ remarks in hand-labelled data, which Chapter 20 meets in the CEO departure notes.

**Using a dictionary from another domain.** A general-purpose list of negative words calls “liability”, “tax” and “crude” negative, which in a 10-K they are not. Either use a list built for your domain or validate a general one against human labels in your domain.

**Measuring the wrong concept.** Tone is not stance, and sentiment is not uncertainty. The net-tone dictionary separated hawkish from dovish sentences with an AUC of only 0.60, because “positive” is not a monetary-policy concept. Name $V$ first and choose the method second.

**Ignoring negation and context.** “Inflation has not declined” counts as a negative-tone sentence. Bigrams help a little, and the contextual models of Chapter 21 help a lot, but a bag of words will never read a sentence.

**Trusting a learned dictionary without reading it.** The rental LASSO kept bathroom counts, foreign words for “picture” and realtor names. None of these show up in an $R^2$. Print the coefficients, and for any surprising word, read five documents that contain it.

**Tuning the pipeline on the test set.** There are dozens of preprocessing choices (case, stopwords, stemming, n-grams, `min_df`), and trying them all and reporting the best test score is the text version of specification searching. Choose them by cross-validation on training data, or fix them in advance, and report a few alternatives.

## Exercises

1.  The EPU filter requires an article to contain a term from *each* of the E, P and U lists. Explain what would go wrong with an index that counted articles containing just “uncertain” or “uncertainty”. What would go wrong if it required only E and U?

2.  Loughran and McDonald found that words like “liability” and “cost” are not negative in 10-Ks. Give an example of a word that would be misclassified by a general-purpose sentiment dictionary in FOMC text, and explain why.

3.  Using the illustrative `lm` word lists, compute the share of FOMC training sentences of each stance (hawkish, dovish, neutral) that contain at least one *uncertainty* word. Do neutral sentences sound more uncertain than the others?

4.  Fit the LASSO logistic regression on all **three** classes (dovish, hawkish, neutral) using the same tf-idf settings. Report test accuracy and compare it with the majority-class baseline. Is the gain over the baseline larger or smaller than in the two-class problem?

5.  Preprocessing robustness: refit the two-class FOMC model with unigrams only (`ngram_range=(1, 1)`), and separately with `min_df=5`. Report the test accuracy of each next to the original. What would you write in a paper’s robustness section?

6.  Extend the leak regex to also remove bathroom counts such as “2ba” or “1.5ba”. What share of the cleaned San Francisco titles contained one? Refit the text-only LASSO with the chosen penalty and report the change in test $R^2$.

7.  A city housing office wants to use the text model to flag “overpriced” listings: ads whose rent is far above the model’s prediction. What would you need to show them first? Think about leakage, time and what the residual measures.

### Answers

**1.** “Uncertain” alone catches every article about uncertain weather, uncertain playoffs and uncertain elections, so the index would mostly measure the volume of news, not policy uncertainty. Requiring E and U but not P catches uncertainty about the economy for any reason (a hurricane, an oil shock, a bank failure), which is economic uncertainty in general rather than uncertainty about *policy*. The three-way filter is what makes the index specific to the concept in its name, and the human audit is how Baker, Bloom and Davis checked that it is.

**2.** One example is “unemployment”, which a general dictionary might treat as negative. In FOMC text the word appears in hawkish sentences (“the unemployment rate has declined to a low level”) and dovish ones (“unemployment remains elevated”) alike, because it is the name of one half of the Fed’s mandate. Another is “accommodation” or “accommodative”: neutral or even warm in ordinary English, but a technical term for easy policy. A domain changes what words mean.

**3.**

``` python
unc = train.assign(any_unc=[any(t in lm["uncertainty"] for t in tokens(s)) for s in train["sentence"]])
unc_share = unc.groupby("stance")["any_unc"].mean()
print(unc_share.round(3).to_string())
```

    stance
    dovish     0.188
    hawkish    0.189
    neutral    0.165

19% of dovish, 19% of hawkish and 16% of neutral sentences contain an uncertainty word. The three shares are within 2 percentage points of each other, so no: by this measure neutral sentences do not sound more uncertain than the others, and uncertainty words tell you almost nothing about stance. Note how blunt the instrument is: “may” and “could” are on the list, and they are as much grammar as doubt. A real test would use the full Loughran–McDonald uncertainty list and check a sample of sentences by hand.

**4.**

``` python
from sklearn.multiclass import OneVsRestClassifier     # liblinear fits two classes at a time
three = make_pipeline(TfidfVectorizer(ngram_range=(1, 2), min_df=2, sublinear_tf=True),
                      OneVsRestClassifier(LogisticRegressionCV(Cs=10, cv=5, penalty="l1", solver="liblinear",
                                                               scoring="accuracy", random_state=SEED)))
three.fit(train["sentence"], train["label"])
acc3 = accuracy_score(test["label"], three.predict(test["sentence"]))
base3 = test["label"].value_counts(normalize=True).max()
print(round(acc3, 3), round(base3, 3))
```

    0.609 0.498

Three-class accuracy is 60.9% against a baseline of 49.8% (always “neutral”). The gain is 11 percentage points, against 19 in the two-class problem. Smaller: neutral is the hardest class to pin down with words, because a neutral sentence is defined by what it does not say.

**5.**

``` python
def acc_with(**kw):
    m = make_pipeline(TfidfVectorizer(sublinear_tf=True, **kw),
                      LogisticRegressionCV(Cs=10, cv=5, penalty="l1", solver="liblinear",
                                           scoring="accuracy", random_state=SEED))
    return accuracy_score(bin_te["label"], m.fit(bin_tr["sentence"], bin_tr["label"]).predict(bin_te["sentence"]))
rob = {"original (1-2 grams, min_df=2)": acc_lasso,
       "unigrams only": acc_with(ngram_range=(1, 1), min_df=2),
       "min_df=5": acc_with(ngram_range=(1, 2), min_df=5)}
print(pd.Series(rob).round(3).to_string())
```

    original (1-2 grams, min_df=2)    0.731
    unigrams only                     0.759
    min_df=5                          0.731

The three accuracies are 73.1%, 75.9%, 73.1%. With 249 test sentences, one sentence is 0.4% of accuracy, so the largest gap, 2.8 points, is about 7 sentences. A paper would report the whole table and say that performance moves by a few sentences across reasonable pipelines, which is within sampling noise. What it must not do is pick the best-looking variant *because* it scored best on the test set: that turns the test set into a training set, and the reported accuracy would be optimistic.

**6.**

``` python
BA = r"\b\d(?:\.\d)?\s?ba\b"
share_ba = sf["text"].str.contains(BA).mean()
tr2, te2 = tr["text"].str.replace(BA, " ", regex=True), te["text"].str.replace(BA, " ", regex=True)
v2 = TfidfVectorizer(min_df=10, ngram_range=(1, 2), sublinear_tf=True, stop_words="english")
m2 = Lasso(alpha=text_only.alpha_, max_iter=3000).fit(v2.fit_transform(tr2), tr["log_price"] - ybar)
r2_noba = r2_score(te["log_price"], m2.predict(v2.transform(te2)) + ybar)
print(round(share_ba, 3), round(r2_text, 3), round(r2_noba, 3))
```

    0.1 0.517 0.509

10.0% of cleaned San Francisco titles contained a bathroom count. Removing them moves the text-only test $R^2$ from 0.517 to 0.509. Whether that is “leakage” is a judgement: the number of bathrooms is a genuine feature of the flat, and a housing office would know it. The problem is only that it is not what we meant by “what the words add”. If you want to measure that, put bathrooms in the structured model and take them out of the text.

**7.** At least four things. First, that the model contains no leak: no prices, and ideally no bedroom or bathroom counts in the text, which belong in the structured part. Second, that it predicts well on listings from a later period than it was trained on (a time split, as in Chapter 12’s exercise), since rents and Craigslist’s page format both changed over the years and words like “resim” are calendar proxies. Third, what the residual actually measures: a listing is “overpriced” relative to the model only if the model sees everything that justifies a high rent, and a one-line title does not mention the renovated kitchen. A large residual is as likely to be an unmeasured amenity as an overcharge. Fourth, who would be flagged: if residuals are systematically larger in some neighbourhoods or for some kinds of landlord, the tool would target them, and the office should see the error rates by group before using it.

## Economics reading

**Gentzkow, Matthew, Bryan Kelly and Matt Taddy (2019). “Text as Data.” *Journal of Economic Literature* 57(3): 535–574.** [doi.org/10.1257/jel.20181020](https://doi.org/10.1257/jel.20181020)

The survey that gave this chapter its framework. The authors set out the represent, measure, use pipeline, explain why text is a high-dimensional problem, and walk through dictionaries, penalised regressions, topic models and word embeddings with examples from finance, macroeconomics and political economy. The early sections are readable with nothing more than this chapter and Chapter 11.

*Think about:* the authors stress that $\hat V$ is usually an input to a second-stage economic analysis. If the errors in $\hat V$ are correlated with your treatment variable, what happens to your estimate, and which of this chapter’s validation habits would detect it?

**Baker, Scott R., Nicholas Bloom and Steven J. Davis (2016). “Measuring Economic Policy Uncertainty.” *Quarterly Journal of Economics* 131(4): 1593–1636.** [doi.org/10.1093/qje/qjw024](https://doi.org/10.1093/qje/qjw024)

The paper behind the EPU index. Baker, Bloom and Davis build the index from three short word lists applied to ten US newspapers, validate it against a large human audit of articles, and extend it to other countries and to specific policy areas. They then show that firms more exposed to government purchases cut investment and employment when policy uncertainty rises, and that at the aggregate level spikes in the index foreshadow declines in investment, output and employment.

*Think about:* the index rises when newspapers *write* more about policy uncertainty. Can you think of a reason newspapers might write more about uncertainty when the economy is already weakening for other reasons? What would that do to the paper’s claim that uncertainty causes firms to cut investment?

**Shah, Agam, Suvan Paturi and Sudheer Chava (2023). “Trillion Dollar Words: A New Financial Dataset, Task & Market Analysis.” *Proceedings of the 61st Annual Meeting of the Association for Computational Linguistics (Volume 1: Long Papers)*: 6664–6679.** [doi.org/10.18653/v1/2023.acl-long.368](https://doi.org/10.18653/v1/2023.acl-long.368)

The source of our FOMC sentences. The authors collect FOMC statements, minutes and speeches, have them labelled hawkish, dovish or neutral, and compare classifiers from simple word-count models up to large language models on the task.
